82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathchoice{D^{{{j}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{D^{{{j}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{D^{{{j}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{D^{{{j}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathchoice{E}{E}{E}{E}\big)\Big).

(3.16)

Now, adding the boundary term contributions
and subtracting the contributions from the second-order perturbations
\mathchoice{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.75381pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5384pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\nu\rho\sigma}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.75381pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5384pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\nu\rho\sigma}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.75381pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5384pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\nu\rho\sigma}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.75381pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5384pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\nu\rho\sigma}}}}
, we find that the difference is given by

\displaystyle\delta^{2}S_{E}^{\text{boundary}}=\int\differential{r}\differential[3]{x}\,a^{2}\sqrt{\gamma}e^{b\varphi}\Big((\delta\mathchoice{\dot{\chi}}{\dot{\chi}}{\dot{\chi}}{\dot{\chi}})^{2}-\dot{\chi}\delta\mathchoice{\dot{\chi}}{\dot{\chi}}{\dot{\chi}}{\dot{\chi}}\left(\mathchoice{A}{A}{A}{A}+3\mathchoice{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}-\triangle\mathchoice{E}{E}{E}{E}\right)-\dot{\chi}\mathchoice{D^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}{D^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}{D^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}{D^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}\delta\mathchoice{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}\mathchoice{D^{{{i}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{D^{{{i}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{D^{{{i}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{D^{{{i}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathchoice{B}{B}{B}{B}+\mathchoice{D^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}{D^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}{D^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}{D^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}\delta\mathchoice{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}\mathchoice{D^{{{i}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{D^{{{i}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{D^{{{i}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{D^{{{i}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\delta\mathchoice{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}+b\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}\dot{\chi}\delta\mathchoice{\dot{\chi}}{\dot{\chi}}{\dot{\chi}}{\dot{\chi}}\Big).

(3.17)

Using the transformations derived from the Hodge duality rules (
3.13
)

\displaystyle\dot{\chi}

\displaystyle\to\frac{e^{-b\varphi}Q}{a^{2}},

\displaystyle\delta\mathchoice{\dot{\chi}}{\dot{\chi}}{\dot{\chi}}{\dot{\chi}}

\displaystyle\to\frac{e^{-b\varphi}}{a^{2}}\left(f+Q\left(\mathchoice{A}{A}{A}{A}+3\mathchoice{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}-\triangle\mathchoice{E}{E}{E}{E}-b\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}\right)\right),

\displaystyle\mathchoice{D^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}{D^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}{D^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}{D^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}\delta\mathchoice{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}

\displaystyle\to\frac{e^{-b\varphi}}{a^{2}}\left(\mathchoice{D^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}{D^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}{D^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}{D^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}w+Q\mathchoice{D^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}{D^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}{D^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}{D^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}\mathchoice{B}{B}{B}{B}\right),

(3.18)

we find that both formulations are equivalent, i.e.

\delta^{2}S_{E}^{axion,F}=\delta^{2}S_{E}^{axion,\chi}+\delta^{2}S_{E}^{boundary}.

(3.19)

3.2
Constraints and gauge invariance

3.2.1
Two-form formulation

The total perturbed action in the two-form formulation contains eight scalar degrees of freedom, given by the fields
\mathchoice{A}{A}{A}{A},\mathchoice{B}{B}{B}{B},\mathchoice{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}{\psi},\mathchoice{E}{E}{E}{E},\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi},\delta\mathchoice{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}{\chi},f\text{ and }w
. However, (
3.7
) relates
f
and
w
, and Hodge duality in turn relates this to
\delta\mathchoice{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}
, leaving us with six scalar degrees of freedom. The constraints and gauge freedom reduce this further to two physical degrees of freedom, as we now show.

At this point it is both customary and convenient to switch to Fourier space, by expanding the scalar perturbation variables in spherical harmonics
Y_{nlm}
that are eigenfunctions of the Laplacian
\triangle
on
S^{3}
with eigenvalues
-n^{2}+1
, with
n\in\mathbb{N}
. For simplicity, we drop the subscript
n
on the fluctuation variables.

Integrating out the Lagrange multiplier
\delta\mathchoice{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}
imposes the closure of
\mathchoice{F}{F}{F}{F}
and allows us to replace
w
with
\dot{f}/\triangle
. Below we will compare the two-form fluctuation with the scalar one to find them equal.

The combined quadratic action governing perturbation modes is then given by
6
6

6

Because of the
\dot{f}/\triangle
term, the action below seems to be badly-defined for
n=1
. As we will see, it becomes well-defined once all the constraints have been taken into account. Separate analysis of the
n=1
mode starting from (
3.15
) with the constraint in (
3.7
) just being given by
\dot{f}=0
, yields the identical result. This separate analysis closely resembles the work in
[
11
]
.

\displaystyle\delta^{2}S_{E}=\int\differential{r}\differential[3]{x}\,\sqrt{\gamma}\bigg[\frac{e^{-b\varphi}}{a^{2}}\Big(

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(f^{2}-(\dot{f})^{2}/\triangle\right)+Q\left(f\left(\mathchoice{A}{A}{A}{A}+3\mathchoice{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}-\triangle\mathchoice{E}{E}{E}{E}-b\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}\right)+\mathchoice{B}{B}{B}{B}\dot{f}\right)+\frac{1}{4}Q^{2}b^{2}\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}Q^{2}\left(\left(\mathchoice{A}{A}{A}{A}+3\mathchoice{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}-\triangle\mathchoice{E}{E}{E}{E}\right)^{2}-b\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}\left(\mathchoice{A}{A}{A}{A}+3\mathchoice{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}-\triangle\mathchoice{E}{E}{E}{E}\right)-\mathchoice{B}{B}{B}{B}\triangle\mathchoice{B}{B}{B}{B}+\mathchoice{B}{B}{B}{B}\dot{f}\right)\Big)

\displaystyle+a^{2}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\left((\delta\mathchoice{\dot{\varphi}}{\dot{\varphi}}{\dot{\varphi}}{\dot{\varphi}})^{2}-\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}\triangle\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}\right)+\dot{\varphi}\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}\left(3\mathchoice{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}}+\triangle\left(\mathchoice{B}{B}{B}{B}-\mathchoice{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}\right)\right)-\mathchoice{A}{A}{A}{A}\dot{\varphi}\delta\mathchoice{\dot{\varphi}}{\dot{\varphi}}{\dot{\varphi}}{\dot{\varphi}}

\displaystyle+\mathchoice{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}\triangle\left(\mathchoice{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}-2\mathchoice{A}{A}{A}{A}\right)-3(\mathchoice{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}})^{2}-6\mathcal{H}\mathchoice{A}{A}{A}{A}\mathchoice{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}}-2\left(\mathcal{H}\mathchoice{A}{A}{A}{A}+\mathchoice{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}}\right)\triangle\left(\mathchoice{B}{B}{B}{B}-\mathchoice{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}\right)

\displaystyle+k\left(3\mathchoice{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}^{2}-6\mathchoice{A}{A}{A}{A}\mathchoice{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}-3\mathchoice{A}{A}{A}{A}^{2}+\left(\mathchoice{B}{B}{B}{B}-\mathchoice{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}\right)\triangle\left(\mathchoice{B}{B}{B}{B}-\mathchoice{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}\right)\right)\Big)\bigg]

(3.20)

To identify the constraints induced by the non-dynamical fields
\mathchoice{A}{A}{A}{A}
and
\mathchoice{B}{B}{B}{B}
, we write the quadratic action in Hamiltonian form,

\displaystyle S=\int\differential{r}\left[p_{i}\dot{q}_{i}-H(p,q)-C_{i}G^{i}(p,q)\right],

(3.21)

where
C_{i}
’s are the Lagrange multipliers and
G^{i}(p,q)
are the constraints they imply.

The conjugate momenta of the dynamical variables
\mathchoice{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}{\psi},\mathchoice{E}{E}{E}{E},f\text{ and }\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}
are given by

\displaystyle\Pi_{f}

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-b\varphi}}{\triangle a^{2}}\left(Q\triangle\mathchoice{B}{B}{B}{B}-\dot{f}\right),

\displaystyle\Pi_{\psi}

\displaystyle=a^{2}\left(3\dot{\varphi}\delta\mathchoice{\dot{\varphi}}{\dot{\varphi}}{\dot{\varphi}}{\dot{\varphi}}-6\mathchoice{A}{A}{A}{A}\mathcal{H}-6\mathchoice{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}}+2\triangle\left(\mathchoice{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}-\mathchoice{B}{B}{B}{B}\right)\right),

\displaystyle\Pi_{\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}}

\displaystyle=a^{2}\left(\delta\mathchoice{\dot{\varphi}}{\dot{\varphi}}{\dot{\varphi}}{\dot{\varphi}}-\dot{\varphi}\mathchoice{A}{A}{A}{A}\right),

\displaystyle\Pi_{E}

\displaystyle=\triangle a^{2}\left(-\dot{\varphi}\delta\mathchoice{\dot{\varphi}}{\dot{\varphi}}{\dot{\varphi}}{\dot{\varphi}}+2\mathchoice{A}{A}{A}{A}\mathcal{H}+2\mathchoice{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}}+2k\left(\mathchoice{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}-\mathchoice{B}{B}{B}{B}\right)\right).

(3.22)

Substituting these in the action yields

\displaystyle\delta^{2}S_{E}=

\displaystyle\int\differential{r}\bigg[\Pi_{\psi}\mathchoice{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}}+\Pi_{E}\mathchoice{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}+\Pi_{f}\dot{f}+\Pi_{\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}}\delta\mathchoice{\dot{\varphi}}{\dot{\varphi}}{\dot{\varphi}}{\dot{\varphi}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{4\left(\triangle+3k\right)a^{2}}\left(k\Pi_{\psi}^{2}-\frac{3}{\triangle}\Pi_{E}^{2}-2\Pi_{\psi}\Pi_{E}\right)+\frac{a^{2}}{2}e^{b\varphi}\triangle\Pi_{f}^{2}+\frac{1}{2a^{2}}\Pi_{\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\dot{\varphi}\Pi_{\psi}\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}+a^{2}\left(\triangle+3k\right)\psi^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{e^{-b\varphi}}{2a^{2}}\left(f+Q(3\mathchoice{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}-\triangle\mathchoice{E}{E}{E}{E}-b\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi})\right)^{2}-\frac{e^{-b\varphi}}{4a^{2}}Q^{2}b^{2}\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}^{2}+\frac{1}{2a^{2}}\left(\frac{3}{2}(\dot{\varphi})^{2}-\triangle\right)\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}^{2}-\mathchoice{B}{B}{B}{B}\left(\Pi_{E}+Q\triangle\Pi_{f}\right)

\displaystyle-\mathchoice{A}{A}{A}{A}\left(\Pi_{\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}}\dot{\varphi}-\mathcal{H}\Pi_{\psi}+\frac{Q\,e^{-b\varphi}}{a^{2}}\left(Q(\triangle\mathchoice{E}{E}{E}{E}-3\mathchoice{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}+\frac{1}{2}b\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi})-f\right)+a^{2}\left(3\mathcal{H}\dot{\varphi}\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}+2(\triangle+3k)\mathchoice{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}\right)\right)\bigg]

(3.23)

which gives rise to the following two constraint equations, associated resp. with
\mathchoice{A}{A}{A}{A}
and
\mathchoice{B}{B}{B}{B}
,

\displaystyle\Pi_{\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}}\dot{\varphi}-\mathcal{H}\Pi_{\psi}+\frac{Q\,e^{-b\varphi}}{a^{2}}\left(Q(\triangle\mathchoice{E}{E}{E}{E}-3\mathchoice{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}+\frac{1}{2}b\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi})-f\right)+a^{2}\left(3\mathcal{H}\dot{\varphi}\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}+2(\triangle+3k)\mathchoice{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}\right)=0

(3.24)

\displaystyle\Pi_{E}+Q\triangle\Pi_{f}=0.

(3.25)

Finally, to resolve the gauge redundancies we introduce the gauge-invariant variables

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle=f-Q\triangle E,

\displaystyle\Phi

\displaystyle=\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}+\frac{\psi}{\mathcal{H}}\dot{\varphi}

(3.26)

Here
R
is defined as in
[
27
]
for the three-form fluctuations, and
\Phi
is a gauge-invariant Mukhanov variable
[
39
,
40
]
. Their conjugate momenta are

\displaystyle\Pi_{R}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{f}+\frac{Qe^{-b\varphi}}{a^{2}\mathcal{H}}\psi,

\displaystyle\Pi_{\Phi}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}}-\left(\frac{be^{-b\varphi}}{2a^{2}\mathcal{H}}+3a^{2}\dot{\varphi}\right)\mathchoice{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}.

(3.27)

The sought-after quadratic action in terms of
R
and
\Phi
is given by

\displaystyle\delta^{2}S_{E}=

\displaystyle\int\differential{r}\Bigg[\Pi_{R}\dot{R}+\Pi_{\Phi}\dot{\Phi}+\Pi_{R}\left(-\frac{e^{-b\varphi}Q^{2}\triangle}{2a^{4}\mathcal{H}(\triangle+3k)}R+\frac{Q\triangle}{4(\triangle+3k)}\left(\frac{be^{-b\varphi}Q^{2}}{a^{4}\mathcal{H}}+6\dot{\varphi}\right)\Phi\right)

\displaystyle+\Pi_{\Phi}\left(\frac{Q\triangle\dot{\varphi}}{2a^{2}\mathcal{H}\left(\triangle+3k\right)}\Pi_{R}-\frac{e^{-b\varphi}Qk\dot{\varphi}}{2a^{4}\mathcal{H}^{2}(\triangle+3k)}R+\frac{\dot{\varphi}\left(be^{-b\varphi}Q^{2}k-2a^{4}\mathcal{H}\triangle\dot{\varphi}\right)}{4a^{4}\mathcal{H}^{2}(\triangle+3k)}\Phi\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{\triangle\left(-3Q^{2}+2e^{b\varphi}a^{4}(\triangle+3k)\right)}{4a^{2}(\triangle+3k)}\Pi_{R}^{2}+\frac{e^{-2b\varphi}\left(-Q^{2}\triangle+e^{b\varphi}a^{4}(\triangle+3k)\left(6k+(\dot{\varphi})^{2}\right)\right)}{12a^{6}\mathcal{H}^{2}(\triangle+3k)}R^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{e^{-2b\varphi}\left(bQ^{3}(2\triangle+3k)-2e^{b\varphi}Qa^{4}\left(-3\mathcal{H}\triangle\dot{\varphi}+b(\triangle+3k)(6k+(\dot{\varphi})^{2})\right)\right)}{12a^{6}\mathcal{H}^{2}(\triangle+3k)}R\Phi

\displaystyle+\frac{e^{-2b\varphi}}{48a^{6}\mathcal{H}^{2}(\triangle+3k)}\Big(-b^{2}Q^{4}(2\triangle+3k)-e^{2b\varphi}\triangle a^{8}(6k+(\dot{\varphi})^{2})(2(\triangle+3k)+3(\dot{\varphi})^{2})

\displaystyle+e^{b\varphi}Q^{2}a^{4}(2(\triangle+3k)(\triangle+3b^{2}k)-6b\triangle\mathcal{H}\dot{\varphi}+(3\triangle+b^{2}\triangle+3b^{2}k)(\dot{\varphi})^{2})\Big)\Phi^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{e^{-b\varphi}\left(Q^{2}(\triangle+3k)-e^{b\varphi}a^{4}(6k(\triangle+3k)+\triangle(\dot{\varphi})^{2})\right)}{12a^{6}\mathcal{H}^{2}(\triangle+3k)}\Pi_{\Phi}^{2}\Bigg].

(3.28)

3.2.2
Scalar-field formulation

A similar procedure yields the perturbed action in the scalar-field formulation. Combining the gravity with the field sector, and applying the equations of motion (
3.3
) we obtain, up to a total derivative in
r
and a total derivative on the induced metric, the total perturbed action

\displaystyle\delta^{2}S_{E}=\int\differential{r}\differential[3]{x}a^{2}\sqrt{\gamma}\Bigg[

\displaystyle e^{b\varphi}\Big(-\frac{1}{2}\left(\delta\mathchoice{\dot{\chi}}{\dot{\chi}}{\dot{\chi}}{\dot{\chi}}^{2}-\delta\mathchoice{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}\triangle\delta\mathchoice{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}\right)+\dot{\chi}\delta\mathchoice{\dot{\chi}}{\dot{\chi}}{\dot{\chi}}{\dot{\chi}}\left(\mathchoice{A}{A}{A}{A}-b\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}\right)-\dot{\chi}\delta\mathchoice{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}\left(3\mathchoice{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}}+\triangle(\mathchoice{B}{B}{B}{B}-\mathchoice{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}\right)+\frac{1}{4}b\dot{\chi}^{2}\left(2\mathchoice{A}{A}{A}{A}-b\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}\right)\Big)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\left((\delta\mathchoice{\dot{\varphi}}{\dot{\varphi}}{\dot{\varphi}}{\dot{\varphi}})^{2}-\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}\triangle\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}\right)+\dot{\varphi}\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}\left(3\mathchoice{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}}+\triangle\left(\mathchoice{B}{B}{B}{B}-\mathchoice{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}\right)\right)-\mathchoice{A}{A}{A}{A}\dot{\varphi}\delta\mathchoice{\dot{\varphi}}{\dot{\varphi}}{\dot{\varphi}}{\dot{\varphi}}

\displaystyle+\mathchoice{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}\triangle\left(\mathchoice{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}-2\mathchoice{A}{A}{A}{A}\right)-3(\mathchoice{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}})^{2}-6\mathcal{H}\mathchoice{A}{A}{A}{A}\mathchoice{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}}-2\left(\mathcal{H}\mathchoice{A}{A}{A}{A}+\mathchoice{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}}\right)\triangle\left(\mathchoice{B}{B}{B}{B}-\mathchoice{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}\right)

\displaystyle+k\left(3\mathchoice{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}^{2}-6\mathchoice{A}{A}{A}{A}\mathchoice{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}-3\mathchoice{A}{A}{A}{A}^{2}+\left(\mathchoice{B}{B}{B}{B}-\mathchoice{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}\right)\triangle\left(\mathchoice{B}{B}{B}{B}-\mathchoice{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}\right)\right)\bigg]

(3.29)

The conjugate momenta of the dynamical variables
\psi,\mathchoice{E}{E}{E}{E},\chi\text{ and }\varphi
are

\displaystyle\Pi_{\delta\mathchoice{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}}

\displaystyle=e^{b\varphi}a^{2}\left(A\dot{\chi}-b\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}\dot{\chi}-\delta\mathchoice{\dot{\chi}}{\dot{\chi}}{\dot{\chi}}{\dot{\chi}}\right),

\displaystyle\Pi_{\psi}

\displaystyle=a^{2}\left(3\dot{\varphi}\delta\mathchoice{\dot{\varphi}}{\dot{\varphi}}{\dot{\varphi}}{\dot{\varphi}}-3e^{b\varphi}\dot{\chi}\delta\mathchoice{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}-6\mathchoice{A}{A}{A}{A}\mathcal{H}-6\mathchoice{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}}+2\triangle\left(\mathchoice{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}-\mathchoice{B}{B}{B}{B}\right)\right),

\displaystyle\Pi_{\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}}

\displaystyle=a^{2}\left(\delta\mathchoice{\dot{\varphi}}{\dot{\varphi}}{\dot{\varphi}}{\dot{\varphi}}-\dot{\varphi}\mathchoice{A}{A}{A}{A}\right),

\displaystyle\Pi_{E}

\displaystyle=\triangle a^{2}\left(-\dot{\varphi}\delta\mathchoice{\dot{\varphi}}{\dot{\varphi}}{\dot{\varphi}}{\dot{\varphi}}-e^{b\varphi}\dot{\chi}\delta\mathchoice{\dot{\chi}}{\dot{\chi}}{\dot{\chi}}{\dot{\chi}}+2\mathchoice{A}{A}{A}{A}\mathcal{H}+2\mathchoice{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}}+2k\left(\mathchoice{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}-\mathchoice{B}{B}{B}{B}\right)\right).

(3.30)

When substituted into the action, this yields the action in Hamiltonian form

\displaystyle\delta^{2}S_{E}=

\displaystyle\int\differential{r}\bigg[\Pi_{\psi}\mathchoice{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}}{\dot{\psi}}+\Pi_{E}\mathchoice{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}{\dot{E}}+\Pi_{\delta\mathchoice{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}}\delta\mathchoice{\dot{\chi}}{\dot{\chi}}{\dot{\chi}}{\dot{\chi}}+\Pi_{\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}}\delta\mathchoice{\dot{\varphi}}{\dot{\varphi}}{\dot{\varphi}}{\dot{\varphi}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{4\left(\triangle+3k\right)a^{2}}\left(k\Pi_{\psi}^{2}-\frac{3}{\triangle}\Pi_{E}^{2}-2\Pi_{\psi}\Pi_{E}\right)+\frac{1}{2a^{2}}e^{-b\varphi}\Pi_{\delta\mathchoice{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2a^{2}}\Pi_{\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}}^{2}+\Pi_{\delta\mathchoice{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}}b\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}\dot{\chi}-\frac{1}{2}\Pi_{\psi}\left(\dot{\varphi}\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}-e^{b\varphi}\dot{\chi}\delta\mathchoice{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}\right)

\displaystyle+a^{2}\left(\triangle+3k\right)\psi^{2}+\frac{1}{2}a^{2}\left(\left(\triangle+\frac{3}{2}e^{b\varphi}\dot{\chi}^{2}\right)\delta\mathchoice{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}^{2}+\left(\triangle-\frac{3}{2}\dot{\varphi}^{2}-b^{2}e^{b\varphi}\dot{\chi}^{2}\right)\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}^{2}-3e^{b\varphi}\dot{\varphi}\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}\dot{\chi}\delta\mathchoice{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}\right)

\displaystyle-\mathchoice{B}{B}{B}{B}\Pi_{E}-\mathchoice{A}{A}{A}{A}\left(\Pi_{\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}}\dot{\varphi}+\Pi_{\delta\mathchoice{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}}\dot{\chi}-\mathcal{H}\Pi_{\psi}++a^{2}\left(3\mathcal{H}\dot{\varphi}\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}-3\mathcal{H}e^{b\varphi}\dot{\chi}\delta\mathchoice{\dot{\chi}}{\dot{\chi}}{\dot{\chi}}{\dot{\chi}}-\frac{1}{2}be^{b\varphi}\dot{\chi}^{2}+2(\triangle+3k)\mathchoice{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}\right)\right)\bigg]

(3.31)

and two constraints:

\displaystyle\Pi_{\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}}\dot{\varphi}+\Pi_{\delta\mathchoice{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}}\dot{\chi}-\mathcal{H}\Pi_{\psi}++a^{2}\left(3\mathcal{H}\dot{\varphi}\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}-3\mathcal{H}e^{b\varphi}\dot{\chi}\delta\mathchoice{\dot{\chi}}{\dot{\chi}}{\dot{\chi}}{\dot{\chi}}-\frac{1}{2}be^{b\varphi}\dot{\chi}^{2}+2(\triangle+3k)\mathchoice{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}\right)=0

(3.32)

\displaystyle\Pi_{E}=0.

(3.33)

In terms of the gauge-invariant variables

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}

\displaystyle=\delta\mathchoice{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}+\frac{\psi}{\mathcal{H}}\dot{\chi},

\displaystyle\Phi

\displaystyle=\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}+\frac{\psi}{\mathcal{H}}\dot{\varphi}.

(3.34)

with respective conjugate momenta

\displaystyle\Pi_{\mathcal{X}}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\delta\mathchoice{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}}+3e^{b\varphi}a^{2}\dot{\chi}\mathchoice{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}{\psi},

\displaystyle\Pi_{\Phi}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}}-\left(\frac{be^{-b\varphi}}{2a^{2}\mathcal{H}}+3a^{2}\dot{\varphi}\right)\mathchoice{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}.

(3.35)

the quadratic action reads:

\displaystyle\delta^{2}S_{E}=

\displaystyle\int\differential{r}\Bigg[\Pi_{\mathcal{X}}\dot{\mathcal{X}}+\Pi_{\Phi}\dot{\Phi}+\mathcal{X}\left(\frac{e^{b\varphi}\dot{\chi}^{2}\triangle}{2\mathcal{H}(\triangle+3k)}\Pi_{\mathcal{X}}+\frac{e^{b\varphi}\dot{\chi}\triangle a^{2}}{4(\triangle+3k)}\left(\frac{be^{b\varphi}\dot{\chi}^{2}}{\mathcal{H}}+6\dot{\varphi}\right)\Phi\right)

\displaystyle+\Pi_{\Phi}\left(\frac{e^{b\varphi}\triangle\dot{\chi}\dot{\varphi}}{2\mathcal{H}\left(\triangle+3k\right)}\mathcal{X}+\frac{k\dot{\chi}\dot{\varphi}}{2a^{2}\mathcal{H}^{2}(\triangle+3k)}\Pi_{\mathcal{X}}+\frac{\dot{\varphi}\left(be^{b\varphi}\dot{\chi}^{2}k-2\mathcal{H}\triangle\dot{\varphi}\right)}{4\mathcal{H}^{2}(\triangle+3k)}\Phi\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{e^{b\varphi}a^{2}\triangle\left(2(\triangle+3k)-3e^{b\varphi}\dot{\chi}^{2}\right)}{4(\triangle+3k)}\mathcal{X}^{2}+\frac{e^{-b\varphi}\left((\triangle+3k)\left(6k+\dot{\varphi}^{2}\right)-e^{b\varphi}\triangle\dot{\chi}^{2}\right)}{12a^{2}\mathcal{H}^{2}(\triangle+3k)}\Pi_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}

\displaystyle-\frac{\left(be^{b\varphi}\dot{\chi}^{2}(2\triangle+3k)-2\dot{\chi}\left(-3\mathcal{H}\triangle\dot{\varphi}+b(\triangle+3k)(6k+(\dot{\varphi})^{2})\right)\right)}{12\mathcal{H}^{2}(\triangle+3k)}\Pi_{\mathcal{X}}\Phi

\displaystyle+\frac{a^{2}}{48\mathcal{H}^{2}(\triangle+3k)}\big(-b^{2}e^{2b\varphi}\dot{\chi}^{4}(2\triangle+3k)-\triangle(6k+(\dot{\varphi})^{2})(2(\triangle+3k)+3(\dot{\varphi})^{2})

\displaystyle+2e^{b\varphi}\dot{\chi}^{2}(2(\triangle+3k)(\triangle+3b^{2}k)-6b\triangle\mathcal{H}\dot{\varphi}+\dot{\varphi}^{2}(3\triangle+b^{2}\triangle+3b^{2}k))\big)\Phi^{2}

\displaystyle-\frac{\left((\triangle+3k)(6k-e^{b\varphi}\dot{\chi}^{2})+\triangle(\dot{\varphi})^{2})\right)}{12a^{2}\mathcal{H}^{2}(\triangle+3k)}\Pi_{\Phi}^{2}\Bigg].

(3.36)

By applying (
3.18
) and a symplectic transformation,

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}

\displaystyle\to\Pi_{R},

\displaystyle\Pi_{\mathcal{X}}

\displaystyle\to-R,

(3.37)

including on the
\Pi_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{X}
boundary term, we recover exactly (
3.28
), as expected.

Remember also that eq. (
3.9
) shows that our boundary conditions carry over straightforwardly to boundary conditions on the gauge-invariant variables. For the dilaton gauge-invariant variable
\Phi
we have

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to\pm\infty}\Phi=\lim_{r\to\pm\infty}\left(\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}+\frac{\mathchoice{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}}{\mathcal{H}}\dot{\varphi}\right)=\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi},

(3.38)

Hence, Dirichlet boundary conditions on
\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}
correspond to Dirichlet boundary conditions on
\Phi
.

Next, the condition that the axion charge remains unchanged at the boundary means that
7
7

7

Note that here we again refer to
\triangle
as the Laplacian on the three-sphere, rather than the eigenvalue of the harmonic.

\displaystyle\delta Q_{\text{axion}}=\int_{S^{3}}\differential[3]{x}\sqrt{\gamma}\ f=\int_{S^{3}}\differential[3]{x}\sqrt{\gamma}(R+Q\triangle\mathchoice{E}{E}{E}{E})=\int_{S^{3}}\differential[3]{x}\sqrt{\gamma}\ R

(3.39)

where the total derivative term integrates to zero. Therefore, the absence of axion-charge fluctuations translates to Dirichlet boundary conditions on
R
.

Finally, in the scalar-field formulation, the boundary term from the symplectic transformation implies a Neumann condition on
\mathcal{X}
, or equivalently, a Dirichlet condition on its conjugate momentum
\Pi_{\mathcal{X}}
. Eq. (
3.18
) shows that this provides the correct boundary condition on the axion charge:

\displaystyle\delta Q_{\text{axion}}=\int_{S^{3}}\differential[3]{x}\sqrt{\gamma}\left[a^{2}e^{b\varphi}\delta\mathchoice{\dot{\chi}}{\dot{\chi}}{\dot{\chi}}{\dot{\chi}}-Q\left(3\mathchoice{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}{\psi}+\mathchoice{A}{A}{A}{A}-\triangle\mathchoice{E}{E}{E}{E}-b\delta\mathchoice{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}{\varphi}\right)\right]=-\int_{S^{3}}\differential[3]{x}\sqrt{\gamma}\,\Pi_{\mathcal{X}},

(3.40)

Thus Dirichlet boundary conditions on axion-charge fluctuations correspond to Neuman conditions on
\mathcal{X}
.

3.3
The choice of boundary conditions in
[
1
]

Before we analyse the spectrum of the quadratic action operator, we comment on the subtleties to do with the boundary conditions that have caused so much confusion in previous negative-mode studies.

In
[
1
]
, a stability analysis in the scalar-field formulation appeared to show that axion wormholes have multiple negative modes. By contrast,
[
27
]
found that, based on an analysis in the three-form formalism, axion wormholes have no negative modes. This discrepancy can be traced to the erroneous choice of boundary conditions in
[
1
]
on the gauge-invariant variable that involves the axion. The reason this is a subtle issue is that this gauge-invariant variable is a combination of axion and metric perturbations, which are subject to respectively Neumann and Dirichlet boundary conditions.

To see this explicitly, consider the wormhole solution with the dilaton turned off, viz.
b=0
and
\varphi=0
. The axion gauge-invariant variable and its conjugate used in
[
1
]
were

\begin{split}\mathcal{Y}&=\frac{\mathcal{H}}{\dot{\chi}}\mathcal{X}=\psi+\frac{\mathcal{H}}{\dot{\chi}}\delta\mathchoice{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}\quad,\\
\Pi_{\mathcal{Y}}&=\frac{\dot{\chi}}{\mathcal{H}}\Pi_{\mathcal{X}}=\frac{\dot{\chi}}{\mathcal{H}}\Pi_{\delta\mathchoice{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}{\chi}}+\frac{3a^{2}\dot{\chi}^{2}}{\mathcal{H}}\psi.\end{split}

(3.41)

In terms of these, the perturbed action reads

\delta^{2}S_{E}=\int\mathrm{d}r\left[\Pi_{\mathcal{Y}}\dot{\mathcal{Y}}+C_{1}\Pi_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}+C_{2}\Pi_{\mathcal{Y}}\mathcal{Y}+C_{3}\mathcal{Y}^{2}\right]+\text{boundary terms},

(3.42)

where

\displaystyle C_{1}=\frac{\triangle\mathcal{H}^{2}+3k^{2}(\triangle+3k)}{2\dot{\chi}^{2}a^{2}(\triangle+3k)},

\displaystyle C_{2}=\frac{\triangle\dot{\chi}^{2}-4k(\triangle+3k)}{2(\triangle+3k)\mathcal{H}},

\displaystyle C_{3}=\frac{\triangle a^{2}\dot{\chi}^{2}\left(2(\triangle+3k)-3\dot{\chi}^{2}\right)}{4(\triangle+3k)\mathcal{H}^{2}}.

(3.43)

The fluctuation of axion charge in (
3.40
) in terms of (
3.41
) is given by

\delta Q_{\text{axion}}\ \propto\ \int_{S^{3}}\mathrm{d}^{3}x\sqrt{|\gamma|}\ a^{2}\Pi_{\mathcal{Y}}

(3.44)

Hence the boundary condition that the axion charge remains fixed requires that
\Pi_{\mathcal{Y}}\to 0
faster than
e^{2|r|}
in the asymptotic regions
|r|\to\infty
. It is safe to say indeed that one can obtain the results of
[
27
]
by integrating out
\mathcal{Y}
in (
3.42
), defining the Sturm-Liouville problem of the form

\hat{M}\Pi_{\mathcal{Y}}=\lambda\Pi_{\mathcal{Y}},

(3.45)

and calculating the eigenvalues of eigenfunctions that obey these boundary conditions. However, this was not what was done in
[
1
]
, where an analysis was performed based on an insufficiently precise adaptation of the results in
[
40
]
to wormholes.

Finally, we note that alternatively, one can integrate out the momenta
\Pi_{\mathcal{Y}}
from (
3.42
), which yields

\delta^{2}S=\int\mathrm{d}r\left[-\frac{1}{4C_{1}}\dot{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}+\left(C_{3}-\frac{C_{2}^{2}}{4C_{1}}+\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r}\left(\frac{C_{2}}{4C_{1}}\right)\right)\mathcal{Y}^{2}\right]-\left.\frac{C_{2}}{4C_{1}}\mathcal{Y}^{2}\right|_{\partial}.

(3.46)

Now, the crucial boundary term in this expression was omitted in
[
1
]
. Next a "symplectic flip" of the action was performed by defining the momentum conjugate of
\mathcal{Y}
as
\Pi=-\dot{\mathcal{Y}}/2C_{1}
, yielding

\delta^{2}S_{E}=\int\mathrm{d}r\left[K\dot{\Pi}^{2}+V\Pi^{2}\right]

(3.47)

for some functions
K
and
V
. This expression led
[
1
]
to conclude that wormholes have infinitely many fluctuation modes with Dirichlet boundary conditions on
\Pi
that lower the action. However, given that

\Pi=\Pi_{\mathcal{Y}}+\frac{C_{2}}{2C_{1}}\mathcal{Y}.

(3.48)

it is now clear that 1) the axion charge does not remained unchanged under these fluctuations and 2) they obey Robin boundary conditions on
\mathcal{Y}
, rather than Neumann conditions.

4
Stability analysis

To compute the spectrum of the quadratic action operator that governs the behaviour of linear perturbations around axion-saxion wormholes, we consider the action (
3.28
) in the two-form formulation.
Schematically this reads,

\begin{split}\delta^{2}S_{E}=\int\mathrm{d}r&\bigg[\Pi_{R}\dot{R}+\Pi_{\Phi}\dot{\Phi}+\mathrm{A}_{n}\Pi_{R}^{2}+\mathrm{B}_{n}\Pi_{R}R+\mathrm{C}_{n}R^{2}+\mathrm{D}_{n}\Pi_{\Phi}^{2}+\mathrm{E}_{n}\Pi_{\Phi}\Phi\\
&+\mathrm{F}_{n}\Phi^{2}+\mathrm{G}_{n}\Pi_{\Phi}\Pi_{R}+\mathrm{H}_{n}\Pi_{R}\Phi+\mathrm{I}_{n}\Pi_{\Phi}R+\mathrm{J}_{n}R\Phi\bigg],\end{split}

(4.1)

where we have denoted explicitly the dependence of the terms on the wavenumber
n
, because there are three rather distinct cases to consider:

•

n=1
(
\triangle=0
). This is the homogeneous sector, for which (
4.1
) reduces to

\delta^{2}S^{n=1}_{E}=\int\mathrm{d}r\bigg[\Pi_{R}\dot{R}+\Pi_{\Phi}\dot{\Phi}+\mathrm{C}_{1}R^{2}+\mathrm{D}_{1}\Pi_{\Phi}^{2}+\mathrm{E}_{1}\Pi_{\Phi}\Phi+\mathrm{F}_{1}\Phi^{2}+\mathrm{I}_{1}\Pi_{\Phi}R+\mathrm{J}_{1}R\Phi\bigg].

(4.2)

We see that
\Pi_{R}
is a Lagrange multiplier imposing
\dot{R}=0
. Hence homogeneous fluctuations are non-dynamical in the axion sector. Boundary conditions then imply that
R=0

[
27
]
and we are left with the dilaton sector, governed by

\delta^{2}S^{n=1}_{E}=\int\mathrm{d}r\left[\Pi_{\Phi}\dot{\Phi}+\mathrm{D}_{1}\Pi_{\Phi}^{2}+\mathrm{E}_{1}\Pi_{\Phi}\Phi+\mathrm{F}_{1}\Phi^{2}\right].

(4.3)

To analyse the spectrum of this sector it proves convenient to bring (
4.3
) back into second-order form. Integrating out the momenta yields the following constraint

\dot{\Phi}+2\mathrm{D}_{1}\Pi_{\Phi}+\mathrm{E}_{1}\Phi=0.

(4.4)

which, when substituted, gives the second-order form of the action

\delta^{2}S^{n=1}_{E}=\int\mathrm{d}r\left[-\frac{1}{4\mathrm{D}_{1}}\dot{\Phi}^{2}-\frac{\mathrm{E}_{1}}{2\mathrm{D}_{1}}\dot{\Phi}\Phi+\left(\mathrm{F}_{1}-\frac{\mathrm{E}_{1}^{2}}{4\mathrm{D}_{1}}\right)\Phi^{2}\right],

(4.5)

where

\begin{split}&\mathrm{D}_{1}=\frac{e^{-b\varphi}Q^{2}-6a^{4}k}{12a^{6}\mathcal{H}^{2}},\quad\mathrm{E}_{1}=\frac{be^{-b\varphi}Q^{2}\dot{\varphi}}{12a^{4}\mathcal{H}^{2}},\\
&\mathrm{F}_{1}=\frac{b^{2}e^{-2b\varphi}Q^{2}(-Q^{2}+2e^{b\varphi}a^{4}(6k+\dot{\varphi}^{2}))}{48a^{6}\mathcal{H}}.\end{split}

(4.6)

Hence in contrast with axion wormholes without a dilaton field turned on, the dilaton renders the homogeneous mode dynamical. We discuss this sector below in Section
4.1
.

•

n=2
(
\triangle=-3k
): All the terms in (
4.1
) are divergent. This mode is non-dynamical, since it requires infinite action to excite
[
34
]
. That said,
[
27
]
argued that for axion wormholes,
\psi
and
E
form a new dynamical field
\psi+E
. However, it turns out that the action of this mode is a total-derivative term which moreover vanishes because of the boundary conditions, leading back to the conclusion that this mode is non-dynamical.

For all other values of
n
, fluctuations are dynamical in both the axion and dilaton sectors. Again, we impose Dirichlet boundary conditions on
R
and
\Phi
, thus we have to integrate out their momenta in order to bring the action back into second-order form. From the action in (
4.1
), we can easily see that integrating out momenta will impose the following linear constraints

\begin{pmatrix}2\mathrm{A}_{n}&\mathrm{G}_{n}\\
\mathrm{G}_{n}&2\mathrm{D}_{n}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\Pi_{R}\\
\Pi_{\Phi}\end{pmatrix}=-\begin{pmatrix}\dot{R}+\mathrm{B}_{n}R+\mathrm{H}_{n}\Phi\\
\dot{\Phi}+\mathrm{E}_{n}\Phi+\mathrm{I}_{n}R\end{pmatrix}.

(4.7)

This system of equations is invertible if
4\mathrm{A}_{n}\mathrm{D}_{n}-\mathrm{G}^{2}_{n}\neq 0
which, written out explicitly, says

\frac{\triangle}{12a^{8}\mathcal{H}^{2}}\bigg(\big(2e^{b\varphi}a^{4}-\frac{3Q^{2}}{\triangle+3k}\big)\big(e^{-b\varphi}Q^{2}-\frac{a^{4}(6k(\triangle+3k)+\triangle\dot{\varphi}^{2}}{\triangle+3k}\big)-\frac{3Q^{2}\triangle a^{4}\dot{\varphi}^{2}}{(\triangle+3k)^{2}}\bigg)\neq 0.

(4.8)

Substituting the explicit background solutions for the dilaton and scale factor gives

\begin{split}&\frac{\triangle Q^{2}\lvert c\rvert}{72a^{8}\mathcal{H}^{2}(\triangle+3k)^{2}}\left[(\triangle+3k)\cos^{2}\bigg(\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}b\arctan(\tanh(r))\bigg)\cosh^{2}(2r)-9\right]\\
&\left[6k+\triangle+\triangle\cos\bigg(\sqrt{6}b\arctan(\tanh(r))\bigg)\sec\bigg(\sqrt{\frac{3}{2\kapd}}b \arctan(\tanh(r))\bigg)^{2}-2k(\triangle+3k)\right]\neq 0.\end{split}

(4.9)

Note that the first bracket is never zero, since
\triangle+3k<0
, whereas the second bracket is zero at the wormhole throat
r=0
when
k=1
, which is our case of interest.

Upon integrating out the momenta, we obtain the following action

\delta^{2}S_{E}=\int\mathrm{d}r\left[\mathcal{A}_{n}\dot{R}^{2}+\mathcal{B}_{n}R\dot{R}+\mathcal{C}_{n}R^{2}+\mathcal{D}_{n}\dot{\Phi}^{2}+\mathcal{E}_{n}\dot{\Phi}\Phi+\mathcal{F}_{n}\Phi^{2}+\mathcal{G}_{n}R\Phi+\mathcal{I}_{n}\dot{R}\Phi+\mathcal{J}_{n}R\dot{\Phi}+\mathcal{K}_{n}\dot{R}\dot{\Phi}\right],

(4.10)

where
\mathcal{A}_{n},\dots,\mathcal{K}_{n}
are the functions obtained after inverting (
4.7
). We give their explicit expressions in Appendix
A
.

In fact, since
\mathcal{A}_{n}\sim 1/a^{2}
as
r\rightarrow\pm\infty
, it proves convenient to work with the rescaled perturbation variables

R(r)=\sqrt{\cosh{2r}}\ \mathrm{P}(r).

(4.11)

The modified set of functions in the action (
4.10
) after this rescaling are given in Eq (
A.4
) in Appendix
A
.
Asymptotically,

R(r)\xrightarrow{\lvert r\rvert\rightarrow\infty}e^{\lvert r\rvert}\ \mathrm{P}(r).

(4.12)

Hence the rescaled perturbation
\mathrm{P}(r)
must decay faster than
e^{-\lvert r\rvert}
asymptotically to meet our boundary condition.
Finally, writing (
4.10
) as

\delta^{2}S_{E}=\int\mathrm{d}r\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{P}&\Phi\end{pmatrix}\hat{\mathrm{M}}\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{P}\\
\Phi\end{pmatrix}+\mathrm{boundary}\ \mathrm{terms},

(4.13)

gives us the matrix Sturm-Liouville problem

\hat{\mathrm{M}}\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{P}\\
\Phi\end{pmatrix}=\lambda\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{P}\\
\Phi\end{pmatrix},

(4.14)

where
\hat{\mathrm{M}}
is a self-adjoint matrix differential operator whose matrix entries are

\begin{split}\mathrm{M}_{11}&=-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\left(\mathcal{A}_{n}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\right)+\mathcal{C}_{n}-\frac{1}{2}\dot{\mathcal{B}_{n}},\\
\mathrm{M}_{12}&=-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{K}_{n}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}r^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}(\mathcal{J}_{n}-\mathcal{I}_{n}-\dot{\mathcal{K}_{n}})\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathcal{G}_{n}-\dot{\mathcal{I}_{n}}\right),\\
\mathrm{M}_{21}&=-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{K}_{n}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}r^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}(\mathcal{I}_{n}-\mathcal{J}_{n}-\dot{\mathcal{K}_{n}})\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathcal{G}_{n}-\dot{\mathcal{J}_{n}}\right),\\
\mathrm{M}_{22}&=-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\left(\mathcal{D}_{n}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\right)+\mathcal{F}_{n}-\frac{1}{2}\dot{\mathcal{E}_{n}}.\end{split}

(4.15)

The boundary terms in the action are explicitly given by

\bigg\{\mathcal{A}_{n}\mathrm{P}\dot{\mathrm{P}}+\frac{\mathcal{B}_{n}}{2}\mathrm{P}^{2}+\mathcal{D}_{n}\Phi\dot{\Phi}+\frac{\mathcal{E}_{n}}{2}\Phi^{2}+\frac{\mathcal{K}_{n}}{2}(\mathrm{P}\dot{\Phi}+\dot{\mathrm{P}}\Phi)+\frac{1}{2}(\mathcal{I}_{n}+\mathcal{J}_{n})\mathrm{P}\Phi\bigg\}\bigg\rvert_{\partial}.

(4.16)

The only non-trivial boundary term is
\mathcal{D}_{n}\Phi\dot{\Phi}
, since asymptotically
\mathcal{D}_{n}\sim a^{2}
. This means that
\Phi
must decay faster than
e^{-\lvert r\rvert}
at the boundary. Thus we obtain a separate Sturm-Liouville problem for each
S^{3}
mode
n>2
, which we now analyse.

4.1
Homogeneous sector

The action (
4.5
) of the homogeneous dilaton sector takes the form

\delta^{2}S_{E}=\int\mathrm{d}r\left[\alpha\dot{\Phi}^{2}+\beta\dot{\Phi}\Phi+\gamma\Phi^{2}\right],

(4.17)

where

\begin{split}\alpha&=\frac{\sqrt{|c|}\sinh^{2}(2r)\cosh(2 r)}{2\sqrt{6}\left(\cosh^{2}(2r)-\sec^{2}\left(\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}b\arctan(\tanh(r))\right)\right)},\\
\beta&=\frac{b\sqrt{|c|}\tan\left(\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}b\arctan(\tanh(r))\right)\sec^{2}\left(\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}b\arctan(\tanh(r))\right)}{2\sec^{2}\left(\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}b\arctan(\tanh(r))\right)-2\cosh^{2}(2r)},\\
\gamma&=\frac{\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}b^{2}\sqrt{|c|}\text{sech}(2r)\sec^{2}\left(\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}b\arctan(\tanh(r))\right)\left(\sec^{2}\left(\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}b\arctan(\tanh(r))\right)-2\cosh^{2}(2r)\right)}{4\left(\cosh^{2}(2r)-\sec^{2}\left(\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}b\arctan(\tanh(r))\right)\right)}.\end{split}

(4.18)

(a)
\alpha

(b)
\gamma-\frac{\dot{\beta}}{2}

(c)

Figure 1:
Functions in the homogeneous mode action (
4.19
), for different values of
b
(with
c=-1
).

Not surprisingly, the axion charge
Q
doesn’t enter in this action. Note also that the coefficients
\alpha,\beta
and
\gamma
depend on
c
only via a multiplicative factor
\sqrt{\lvert c\rvert}
. Hence the dependence on the size of the wormhole is rather trivial. The only non-trivial physical parameter is the dilaton coupling
b
.

Now, since
\beta\sim e^{-4\lvert r\rvert}
asymptotically, we can integrate the
\beta\dot{\Phi}\Phi
term in (
4.17
) by parts to obtain

\delta^{2}S_{E}=\int\mathrm{d}r\left[\alpha\dot{\Phi}^{2}+\left(\gamma-\frac{\dot{\beta}}{2}\right)\Phi^{2}\right]+\frac{\beta}{2}\Phi^{2}\bigg\rvert_{\partial}.

(4.19)

The boundary term vanishes since
\Phi
obeys Dirichlet boundary conditions. Further, Figure
1
shows that the coefficient functions (
4.19
) are everywhere positive. Hence homogeneous fluctuations are suppressed. There is no negative mode in this sector, given the boundary conditions of interest.

A numerical analysis of the Sturm-Liouville problem defined from (
4.17
) confirms this. Note that even though the functions
\beta
and
\gamma
are singular at the wormhole throat, the combination entering in the Sturm-Liouville problem is well-behaved, as shown in Figure
1
. The singular behaviour does mean, however, that only odd eigenfunctions contribute to the homogeneous sector, since even eigenfunctions give rise to infinite action.

Note that we did not have to perform any additional Hawking-Perry rotation in our analysis to suppress the homogeneous fluctuations. This shows that we do not encounter the conformal factor problem.

4.2
Spectral analysis of the inhomogeneous modes

The inhomogeneous
n>2
modes are governed by the Sturm-Liouville problem defined in (
4.15
). The coupling terms mean that we must resort to a numerical analysis.
As before, even though many of the individual functions in the quadratic action (
4.10
) are singular at the wormhole throat, the combinations of the functions that enter in the Sturm-Liouville problem are well-behaved. They are shown in Appendix
A
in Figure
4
.

Once again, therefore, this means that only odd eigenfunctions contribute to the physically relevant spectrum of fluctuations.

(a)
n=3

(b)
n=4

Figure 2:
The two lowest odd eigenfunctions of the Sturm-Liouville problem (
n=3,4
) in an axion-dilaton wormhole background, for
Q=0.5
,
b=1
and
c=-1
.

To see that even eigenfunctions have infinite action, consider their contribution to the action from the near-throat region. Since even eigenfunctions are approximately constant near
r=0
, the action integral across a narrow interval
\Delta r=2\epsilon
across the throat is approximately

\delta^{2}S_{E}\approx\int_{-\epsilon}^{\epsilon}\mathrm{d}r\left[\mathcal{C}_{n}\mathrm{P}_{e}^{2}+\mathcal{F}_{n}\Phi_{e}^{2}+\mathcal{G}_{n}\mathrm{P}_{e}\Phi_{e}\right]\ ,

(4.20)

where
\mathrm{P}_{e},\Phi_{e}
are constants and
C,F,G
behave as
1/r^{2}
near
r=0
.
In fact, since

\lim_{r\to 0}\bigg\lvert\frac{\mathcal{G}_{n}}{\sqrt{\mathcal{C}_{n}\mathcal{F}_{n}}}\bigg\lvert\to 2.

(4.21)

it turns out that (
4.20
) is approximately

\delta^{2}S_{E}\approx\int_{-\epsilon}^{\epsilon}\mathrm{d}r\left(\sqrt{\mathcal{C}_{n}}\mathrm{P}_{e}\pm\sqrt{\mathcal{F}_{n}}\Phi_{e}\right)^{2}>0.

(4.22)

Hence all even eigenfunctions are infinitely suppressed in the path integral.

We now turn to odd eigenfunctions. The spectrum of this Sturm-Liouville operator is found using the shooting method. Since odd eigenfunctions behave linearly very near the origin, we consider the small-
r
ansatz

\mathrm{P}(r)\sim\mathrm{P}_{0}r,\quad\Phi\sim\Phi_{0}r,

(4.23)

for some constants
\mathrm{P}_{0}
and
\Phi_{0}
. Next we perform a shooting procedure out to the asymptotic region, where we implement the correct boundary conditions. This shooting procedure is slightly more involved than usual and is explained in detail in Appendix
B
. Here we present the main results. The two lowest (normalized) odd eigenfunctions
8
8

8

For each
n
we have a Sturm-Liouville problem on its own and hence, its own spectrum. It should not come as a surprise then that for all
n>2
, the lowest odd eigenfunction contains only one node. Same result was found in
[
27
]
for axion wormholes.
for
Q=0.5
are shown in Figure
2
. The eigenvalues for the five lowest modes are given in Table
1
, for different values of
Q
.

We see the eigenvalues are all positive, so we can conclude that axion-dilaton wormholes are perturbatively stable.

on

and

is given in Appendix

).
For
b\rightarrow 0
,
\mathrm{P}_{0}/\Phi_{0}
diverges, indicating that axion fluctuations dominate in this limit. In this regime, we can ignore the dilaton fluctuations and the spectrum should agree with the spectrum in axion wormhole backgrounds without dilaton. We confirmed that this is indeed the case. The spectrum of the purely axionic Sturm-Liouville problem agrees with that found in this section in the limit of
b\rightarrow 0
. For consistency, we also verified that our results agree with those in
[
27
]
.

5
Conclusions

We have demonstrated that spherically symmetric Euclidean wormholes sourced by an axion and dilaton field are perturbatively stable. This generalizes and corrects earlier studies. Further, we made explicit how the stability analyses carried out in both Hodge-dual frames are equivalent. Given the subtleties to do with the sign of the kinetic term of the axion scalar upon Wick rotation and Hodge duality, this resolves a certain degree of confusion in the existing literature and elucidates the final result that these solutions are perturbatively stable.

An obvious extension of this work includes an analysis of the stability of wormholes in general sigma models. We believe this is fairly straightforward, since more general wormhole backgrounds of this kind are described by geodesics on the target space. This means one could make use of a local coordinate frame adapted to geodesic curves, the so-called free-falling coordinates. Another extension concerns a stability analysis of wormholes in the presence of a non-zero cosmological constant. For a positive cosmological constant,
[
26
]
showed that axionic wormholes without a dilaton field are perturbatively stable. Given the similarities between wormhole solutions in flat space and in AdS, we similarly expect that our stability results remain qualitatively unchanged in the presence of a negative cosmological constant. Finally, it would be interesting to consider the effect of dilaton masses, which would be relevant in a phenomenological setting where supersymmetry is broken
[
9
,
10
]
9
9

9

In AdS, it is possible for the dilaton to have mass even when SUSY is retained
[
14
]
.
.
In this context
[
11
]
recently demonstrated that several classes of wormhole solutions with dilaton potentials are perturbatively stable, at least with respect to homogeneous perturbations. It would be natural to extend their formalism to non-homogeneous fluctuations.

Evidently the absence of negative modes has consequences for the interpretation of these wormholes as saddle points in the gravitational path integral. In order to resolve the factorization paradox in AdS/CFT it would seem that all wormhole contributions should exactly cancel out. This is known as the “baby universe hypothesis”
[
41
,
23
]
. Further, for Euclidean axion wormholes one may expect that this cancellation should happen within each superselection sector separately, labelled by the discrete axion fluxes
Q
. The perturbative stability of axion wormholes, even in the presence of a dilaton, means that it remains very much unclear how this is realized.

Appendix A
Matrix elements of the two-field Sturm-Liouville problem

In this appendix we present the exact expressions for the functions in the Sturm-Liouville problem in (
4.15
)

\begin{split}\mathcal{A}_{n}&=\frac{(\triangle+3k)Q^{2}a^{2}-e^{b\varphi}a^{6}(6k(\triangle+3k)+\triangle\dot{\varphi}^{2})}{\triangle(3Q^{4}-2e^{b\varphi}(\triangle+12k)Q^{2}a^{4}+2e^{2b\varphi}a^{8}(6k(\triangle+3k)+\triangle\dot{\varphi}^{2}))}\\
\mathcal{B}_{n}&=\frac{6Q^{2}a^{2}\mathcal{H}}{3Q^{4}-2e^{b\varphi}(\triangle+12k)Q^{2}a^{4}+2e^{2b\varphi}a^{8}(6k(\triangle+3k)+\triangle\dot{\varphi}^{2})}\\
\mathcal{C}_{n}&=\frac{e^{-b\varphi}(-Q^{2}+e^{b\varphi}a^{4}(6k+\dot{\varphi}^{2}))(-(\triangle+9k)Q^{2}+e^{b\varphi}a^{4}(6k(\triangle+3k)+\triangle\dot{\varphi}^{2})}{6a^{2}\mathcal{H}^{2}(3Q^{4}-2e^{b\varphi}(\triangle+12k)Q^{2}a^{4}+2e^{2b\varphi}a^{8}(6k(\triangle+3k)+\triangle\dot{\varphi}^{2}))}\\
\mathcal{D}_{n}&=\frac{e^{-b\varphi}(-3Q^{2}+2e^{b\varphi}(\triangle+3k)a^{4})(Q^{2}-e^{b\varphi}a^{4}(6k+\dot{\varphi}^{2}))^{2}}{12a^{2}\mathcal{H}^{2}(3Q^{4}-2e^{b\varphi}(\triangle+12k)Q^{2}a^{4}+2e^{2b\varphi}a^{8}(6k(\triangle+3k)+\triangle\dot{\varphi}^{2}))}\\
\mathcal{E}_{n}&=\frac{e^{-b\varphi}\dot{\varphi}(bQ^{6}(\triangle+3k)-4e^{3b\varphi}\triangle a^{12}\mathcal{H}(\triangle+3k)\dot{\varphi}(6k+\dot{\varphi}^{2})}{4a^{2}\mathcal{H}^{2}(\triangle+3k)(3Q^{4}-2e^{b\varphi}Q^{2}a^{4}(\triangle+12k)+2e^{2b\varphi}a^{8}(6k(\triangle+3k)+\triangle\dot{\varphi}^{2}))}\\
&-\frac{e^{b\varphi}Q^{4}a^{4}(6\triangle\mathcal{H}\dot{\varphi}+b(\triangle+3k)(8k+\dot{\varphi}))}{4a^{2}\mathcal{H}^{2}(\triangle+3k)(3Q^{4}-2e^{b\varphi}Q^{2}a^{4}(\triangle+12k)+2e^{2b\varphi}a^{8}(6k(\triangle+3k)+\triangle\dot{\varphi}^{2}))}\\
&+\frac{2e^{2b\varphi}Q^{2}a^{8}(-18\triangle\mathcal{H}^{3}\dot{\varphi}+bk(\triangle+3k)(6k+\dot{\varphi}^{2})+\triangle\mathcal{H}\dot{\varphi}(2\triangle+24k+3\dot{\varphi}^{2}))}{4a^{2}\mathcal{H}^{2}(\triangle+3k)(3Q^{4}-2e^{b\varphi}Q^{2}a^{4}(\triangle+12k)+2e^{2b\varphi}a^{8}(6k(\triangle+3k)+\triangle\dot{\varphi}^{2}))}\\
\mathcal{G}_{n}&=e^{-2b\varphi}\bigg(\frac{(3bQ^{7}(\triangle+3k)-4e^{3b\varphi}Qa^{12}(\triangle+3k)(6k+\dot{\varphi}^{2}(6bk(\triangle+3k)-3\triangle\mathcal{H}\dot{\varphi}+b\triangle\dot{\varphi}^{2})}{12a^{6}\mathcal{H}^{2}(\triangle+3k)(3Q^{4}-2e^{b\varphi}Q^{2}a^{4}(\triangle+12k)+2e^{2b\varphi}a^{8}(6k(\triangle+3k)+\triangle\dot{\varphi}^{2}))}\\
&-\frac{e^{b\varphi}Q^{5}a^{4}(-18\triangle\mathcal{H}\dot{\varphi}+b(\triangle+3k)(4\triangle+60k+3\dot{\varphi}^{2}))}{12a^{6}\mathcal{H}^{2}(\triangle+3k)(3Q^{4}-2e^{b\varphi}Q^{2}a^{4}(\triangle+12k)+2e^{2b\varphi}a^{8}(6k(\triangle+3k)+\triangle\dot{\varphi}^{2}))}\\
&+\frac{2e^{2b\varphi}Q^{3}a^{8}(54\triangle\mathcal{H}^{3}\dot{\varphi}-3\triangle\mathcal{H}\dot{\varphi}(2\triangle+24k+3\dot{\varphi})}{12a^{6}\mathcal{H}^{2}(\triangle+3k)(3Q^{4}-2e^{b\varphi}Q^{2}a^{4}(\triangle+12k)+2e^{2b\varphi}a^{8}(6k(\triangle+3k)+\triangle\dot{\varphi}^{2}))}\\
&+\frac{b(\triangle+3k)(6k(4\triangle+27k)+(4\triangle+21k)\dot{\varphi^{2}}))}{12a^{6}\mathcal{H}^{2}(\triangle+3k)(3Q^{4}-2e^{b\varphi}Q^{2}a^{4}(\triangle+12k)+2e^{2b\varphi}a^{8}(6k(\triangle+3k)+\triangle\dot{\varphi}^{2}))}\bigg)\\
\mathcal{I}_{n}&=\frac{3Qa^{2}\mathcal{H}(bQ^{4}-36e^{2b\varphi}a^{8}\mathcal{H}k\dot{\varphi}-e^{b\varphi}Q^{2}a^{4}(6bk-6\mathcal{H}\dot{\varphi}+b\dot{\varphi}^{2}))}{(Q^{2}-e^{b\varphi}a^{4}(6k+\dot{\varphi}^{2}))(3Q^{4}-2e^{b\varphi}Q^{2}a^{4}(\triangle+12k)+2e^{2b\varphi}a^{8}(6k(\triangle+3k)+\triangle\dot{\varphi}^{2}))}\\
\mathcal{J}_{n}&=\frac{e^{-b\varphi}Q(\kappa_{4}^{2}Q^{2}-2e^{b\varphi}a^{4}k)\dot{\varphi}(Q^{2}-e^{b\varphi}a^{4}(6k+\dot{\varphi}))}{2a^{2}\mathcal{H}^{2}(3Q^{4}-2e^{b\varphi}(\triangle+12k)Q^{2}a^{4}+2e^{2b\varphi}a^{8}(6k(\triangle+3k)+\triangle\dot{\varphi}^{2}))}\\
\mathcal{K}_{n}&=\frac{6e^{b\varphi}Qa^{6}\mathcal{H}\dot{\varphi}}{3Q^{4}-2e^{b\varphi}Q^{2}a^{4}(\triangle+12k)+2e^{2b\varphi}a^{8}(6k(\triangle+12k)+\triangle\dot{\varphi}^{2})}.\\
\end{split}

(A.1)

The function
\mathcal{F}_{n}
is rather complicated and we write it separately

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}_{n}&=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}e^{-2b\varphi}\bigg(-3b^{2}Q^{8}(2\triangle^{2}+9k\triangle+9k^{2})+e^{b\varphi}Q^{6}a^{4}(\triangle+3k)(-18b^{2}\triangle\mathcal{H}^{2}+2(6\triangle(\triangle+3k)\\
&+b^{2}(2\triangle^{2}+45\triangle k+90k^{2}))-36b\triangle\mathcal{H}\dot{\varphi}+3(6\triangle+b^{2}(4\triangle+3k))\dot{\varphi}^{2}\\
&-4e^{4b\varphi}\triangle a^{16}(\triangle+3k)\big(72k^{2}(\triangle+3k)^{2}+24k(\triangle^{2}+9\triangle k+18k^{2})\dot{\varphi}^{2}\\
&+2(\triangle^{2}-9\triangle\mathcal{H}^{2}+21\triangle k+27k^{2})\dot{\varphi}^{4}+3\triangle\dot{\varphi}^{6}\big)\\
&-2e^{2b\varphi}Q^{4}a^{8}\big(2(\triangle+3k)^{2}(2\triangle(\triangle+21k)+3b^{2}k(4\triangle+27k))+108b\triangle\mathcal{H}^{3}(\triangle+3k)\dot{\varphi}\\
&-18(b^{2}+3)\triangle\mathcal{H}^{2}(\triangle+3k)\dot{\varphi}^{2}+(\triangle+3k)(18\triangle(\triangle+8k)+b^{2}(4\triangle^{2}+51\triangle k+63k^{2}))\dot{\varphi}^{2}\\
&+3\triangle(b^{2}(\triangle+3k)+3(2\triangle+3k))\dot{\varphi}^{2}-6b\triangle\mathcal{H}(\triangle+3k)\dot{\varphi}(2\triangle+24k+3\dot{\varphi}^{2}))\\
&+2e^{3b\varphi}Q^{2}a^{12}(24k(\triangle+3k)^{2}(3b^{2}k(\triangle+3k)+\triangle(2\triangle+15k))\\
&+4(\triangle+3k)(3b^{2}k(2\triangle^{2}+9\triangle k+9k^{2})+3\triangle(2\triangle^{2}+45\triangle k+171k^{2}))\dot{\varphi}^{2}+2\triangle(b^{2}(\triangle+3k)\\
&+9(\triangle^{2}+10\triangle k+12k^{2}))\dot{\varphi}^{2}+9\triangle^{2}\dot{\varphi}^{6}-12b\triangle\mathcal{H}(\triangle+3k)^{2}\dot{\varphi}(6k+\dot{\varphi}^{2})\\
&-18\triangle\mathcal{H}^{2}\dot{\varphi}^{2}(2\triangle^{2}+24\triangle k+54k^{2}+3\triangle\dot{\varphi}^{2})))\bigg),\end{split}

(A.2)

where

\mathcal{N}=48a^{6}\mathcal{H}^{2}(\triangle+3k)^{2}(3Q^{4}-2e^{b\varphi}Q^{2}a^{4}(\triangle+12k)+2e^{2b\varphi}a^{8}(6k(\triangle+3k)+\triangle\dot{\varphi}^{2})).

(A.3)

After the rescaling of the gauge-invariant perturbations (
4.11
) the former functions change to

\begin{split}&\mathcal{A}_{n}\ \rightarrow\ \mathcal{A}_{n}\cosh{2r},\quad\mathcal{B}_{n}\ \rightarrow\ \mathcal{B}_{n}\cosh{2r}+2\mathcal{A}_{n}\sinh{2r},\\
&\mathcal{C}_{n}\ \rightarrow\ \mathcal{C}_{n}\cosh{2r}+\mathcal{B}_{n}\sinh{2r}+\mathcal{A}_{n}\sinh{2r}\tanh{2r}\\
,&\mathcal{G}_{n}\ \rightarrow\ \sqrt{\cosh{2r}}\ \mathcal{G}_{n}+\frac{\sinh{2r}}{\sqrt{\cosh{2r}}}\ \mathcal{I}_{n},\\
&\mathcal{I}_{n}\ \rightarrow\ \sqrt{\cosh{2r}}\mathcal{I}_{n},\quad\mathcal{J}_{n}\ \rightarrow\ \sqrt{\cosh{2r}}\ \mathcal{J}_{n}+\frac{\sinh{2r}}{\sqrt{\cosh{2r}}}\ \mathcal{K}_{n},\quad\mathcal{K}_{n}\ \rightarrow\sqrt{\cosh{2r}}\ \mathcal{K}_{n}.\end{split}

(A.4)

\mathcal{D}_{n},\mathcal{E}_{n},\mathcal{F}_{n}
remain unchanged under this rescaling of perturbations. They are given in Figure
4
.

Appendix B
Numerical method for the odd eigenfunctions

In this appendix we explain the numerical method used to calculate the spectrum in Section
4.2
for odd eigenfunctions. Odd eigenfunctions exhibit a linear behaviour near the origin, so we consider the small-
r
ansatz

\mathrm{P}(r)\sim\mathrm{P}_{0}r,\quad\Phi\sim\Phi_{0}r,

(B.1)

for some constants
\mathrm{P}_{0}
and
\Phi_{0}
and then perform the shooting method to infinity. In the asymptotic regions
r\rightarrow\pm\infty
there will be a combination of decaying and diverging solutions, we want to make sure that the diverging ones vanish consistent with Dirichlet boundary conditions. In order to find asymptotic solutions, we first solve the decoupled differential equations

-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\left(\mathcal{A}_{n}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\right)\mathrm{P}+\left(\mathcal{C}_{n}-\frac{1}{2}\dot{\mathcal{B}_{n}}\right)\mathrm{P}=\lambda\mathrm{P},\quad-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\left(\mathcal{D}_{n}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\right)\Phi+\left(\mathcal{F}_{n}-\frac{1}{2}\dot{\mathcal{E}_{n}}\right)\Phi=0.

(B.2)

There is no term proportional to the eigenvalue in the equation for
\Phi
since
\mathcal{D}_{n}
and
\left(\mathcal{F}_{n}-\frac{1}{2}\dot{\mathcal{E}_{n}}\right)
behave as
e^{2\lvert r\rvert}
asymptotically and they are dominant over that term. The off-diagonal terms arising from the coupling act as sources for the particular solutions when solutions to (
B.2
) are substituted in.
If we define

\alpha_{1}=\sqrt{n^{2}-a_{0}\lambda},\quad\alpha_{2}=\sqrt{1+b_{0}}-1.

(B.3)

where

a_{0}=\lim_{\lvert r\rvert\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\mathcal{A}_{n}}=(n^{2}-1)\sqrt{\frac{2\lvert c\rvert}{3}}e^{b\varphi}\bigg\rvert_{\lvert r\rvert\rightarrow\infty}\quad b_{0}=\lim_{\lvert r\rvert\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\left(\mathcal{F}_{n}-\frac{1}{2}\dot{\mathcal{E}_{n}}\right)=n^{2}-1,

(B.4)

we can simply write the diverging solutions as

\begin{split}&\mathrm{P}(r)\xrightarrow{\lvert r\rvert\rightarrow\infty}\beta_{1}\exp(\alpha_1\lvert r\rvert)+\beta_{2}\mathrm{P}^{\mathrm{div}}_{\mathrm{part}}(\lvert r\rvert),\\
&\Phi(r)\xrightarrow{\lvert r\rvert\rightarrow\infty}\beta_{2}\exp(\alpha_2\lvert r \rvert)+\beta_{1}\Phi^{\mathrm{div}}_{\mathrm{part}}(\lvert r\rvert),\end{split}

(B.5)

where
\beta
’s are integration constants. The term multiplied with an integration constant
\beta_{2}
appears also in the asymptotic expression for
\mathrm{P}
because, since
\beta_{2}\exp(\alpha_2 \lvert r \rvert)
is the solution of the decoupled equation in (
B.2
), it will act as a source for the particular solution in
\mathrm{P}
thus leading to the term proportional to
\beta_{2}
. Similar reasoning holds for the appearance of
\beta_{1}
in the asymptotic expression for
\Phi
.

These particular solutions are given by

\mathrm{P}^{\mathrm{div}}_{\mathrm{part}}(r)=c_{1}e^{(\alpha_{2}-1)r}+c_{2}e^{(\alpha_{2}-3)r},\quad\Phi^{\mathrm{div}}_{\mathrm{part}}(r)=d_{1}e^{(\alpha_{1}-3)r}+d_{2}e^{(\alpha_{1}-5)r},

(B.6)

where
c_{i}
and
d_{i}
are coefficients which are determined numerically. The decaying solutions in the asymptotic regions are

\begin{split}&\mathrm{P}(r)\xrightarrow{\lvert r\rvert\rightarrow\infty}\gamma_{1}\exp\left(-\alpha_{1}\lvert r\rvert\right)+\gamma_{2}\mathrm{P}^{\mathrm{dec}}_{\mathrm{part}}(\lvert r\rvert),\\
&\Phi(r)\xrightarrow{\lvert r\rvert\rightarrow\infty}\gamma_{2}\exp\left(-\left(\alpha_{2}+2\right)\lvert r\rvert\right)+\gamma_{1}\mathrm{P}^{\mathrm{dec}}_{\mathrm{part}}(\lvert r\rvert),\end{split}

(B.7)

where
\gamma
’s are integration constants. The argument for their appearance in both of these asymptotic expressions is identical to the one presented for
\beta
’s above. As one can see from (
B.7
), there are new particular solutions induced by decaying solutions and they are given by
10
10

10

The particular solutions in (
B.5
) and (
B.7
) are not the only ones we can include, e.g. we can do another iteration so that
\Phi_{\mathrm{part}}^{\lambda}
can now induce another solution for
\mathrm{P}
and vice versa. However, asymptotic solutions included in (
B.5
) and (
B.7
) will always contain the most dominant behaviour in the asymptotic regions, thus it is not necessary to include any further solutions that may be induced.

\mathrm{P}^{\mathrm{dec}}_{\mathrm{part}}(r)=c_{3}e^{-(\alpha_{2}+3)r}+c_{4}e^{-(\alpha_{2}+5)r},\quad\Phi^{\mathrm{dec}}_{\mathrm{part}}(r)=d_{3}e^{-(\alpha_{1}+3)r}+d_{4}e^{-(\alpha_{1}+5)r},

(B.8)

where
c_{3,4}
and
d_{3,4}
are again determined numerically.

To perform an appropriate shooting method in two-dimensional Sturm-Liouville problems, we need two parameters which are varied in the shooting. We want integration constants in (
B.5
) to be equal to
\beta_{1}=\beta_{2}=0
, thereby eliminating the divergent solutions. In the shooting code itself, those integration constants are defined as functions of
r
by inverting (
B.5
) such that they approach a constant value asymptotically. There is a precise combination of constants coming from the behaviour near
r=0
, which we will denote as (
\mathrm{P}_{0},\Phi_{0}
), and the eigenvalue
\lambda
which makes them vanish. Moreover, for each value of the parameters (
Q,n\dots
), there will be a
\lambda
for which a certain ratio of
\mathrm{P}_{0}/\Phi_{0}
will eliminate the diverging solutions and impose vanishing boundary conditions on the fluctuations. The extra shooting parameter, along with the eigenvalue
\lambda
, represents the ratio of slopes originating from the linear behaviour of the two gauge-invariant fluctuations near the wormhole throat. This should not come as a surprise. Since the fluctuations are coupled, one could initially expect the importance of a mutual hierarchy between the fluctuations, which makes them source each other out and vanish at infinity.

(a)
n=3

(b)
n=4

Figure 5:
Function
\Delta\mathrm{P}_{0}(\lambda)
as defined in the text, zeroes of this function correspond to the spectrum of the respective inhomogeneous mode. However, one should check that the zeroes do in fact represent the eigenvalues and that they are not an artefact of numerical errors. Sketched for
Q=0.75
,
b=1
and
c=-1
.

Since it is the ratio of
\mathrm{P}_{0}
and
\Phi_{0}
that matters in the shooting method, we have a freedom in setting one of the constants to some value, we choose
\Phi_{0}=1
, and then find the appropriate values (
\lambda,\mathrm{P}_{0}
) which eliminate the diverging solutions. This just means that
\Phi_{0}
has been absorbed in the normalization. The eigenfunctions need to be orthonormal and actual values of
\mathrm{P}_{0}
and
\Phi_{0}
are then determined by normalization.

We perform the shooting in the following way: for a certain
\lambda
we identify

\begin{split}&\mathrm{P}_{0}^{1}:\quad\text{value of }\mathrm{P}_{0}\text{ that makes }\beta_{1}=0,\\
&\mathrm{P}_{0}^{2}:\quad\text{value of }\mathrm{P}_{0}\text{ that makes }\beta_{2}=0,\end{split}

(B.9)

with the FindRoot function in Mathematica. This defines
\Delta\mathrm{P}_{0}=\mathrm{P}_{0}^{1}-\mathrm{P}_{0}^{2}
, which depends on the eigenvalue, i.e.
\Delta\mathrm{P}_{0}(\lambda)
, and it is sketched in Figures
5
for the lowest inhomogeneous modes. We then identify the values
\lambda_{a}
and
\lambda_{b}
for which
\Delta\mathrm{P}_{0}(\lambda)
has different signs. In the interval
[\lambda_{a},\lambda_{b}]
we perform a linear interpolation to find the eigenvalue

\lambda_{c}=\lambda_{b}-\Delta\mathrm{P}_{0}(\lambda_{b})\frac{\lambda_{a}-\lambda_{b}}{\Delta\mathrm{P}_{0}(\lambda_{a})-\Delta\mathrm{P}_{0}(\lambda_{b})},

(B.10)

over a couple of iterations to make
\Delta\mathrm{P}_{0}\approx 0
.
11
11

11

Alternatively, one can introduce another FindRoot function which then determines the value of
\lambda
for which
\Delta\mathrm{P}_{0}(\lambda)\approx 0
.
This determines the eigenvalue and the value of
\mathrm{P}_{0}
. As discussed previously, there will be an upper bound on
\lambda
due to the fact that
\mathrm{P}(r)
must decay faster than
e^{-\lvert r\rvert}
. This would translate to the bound
\alpha_{1}<1
or

\lambda<\sqrt{\frac{3}{2\lvert c\rvert}}e^{-b\varphi}\bigg\rvert_{\lvert r\rvert\rightarrow\infty}=\sqrt{\frac{3\lvert c\rvert}{2}}\frac{1}{Q^{2}\cos^{2}\left(\frac{\pi}{4}\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}b\right)}.

(B.11)

With this bound satisfied, fluctuations also decay consistently as demanded by the non-trivial boundary term
\mathcal{D}_{n}
.

These results can also be confirmed by usual shooting from infinity where the initial conditions are taken as follows. For a certain value of
\lambda
, the decaying solutions in (
B.7
) will give the initial conditions in terms of integration constants
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
. Again, it is the ratio that matters since one of the constants can be absorbed in the normalization, thus we can set one of them equal to one, e.g.
\gamma_{2}=1
. Interpolation is then done as before to determine the value of
\gamma_{1}
which eliminates the diverging solutions in the other asymptotic region. The spectrum and the values of
\mathrm{P}_{0}/\Phi_{0}
agree with Table
1
.

Appendix C
Dependence of shooting parameters on
Q
and
c

In this appendix we show how
\lambda
and
\mathrm{P}_{0}/\Phi_{0}
from Section
4.2
depend on the parameters
Q
and
c
. This is outlined in Figures
7
and
7
. Combining the results from Table
1
and Figure
7
done for the lowest inhomogeneous modes, we can see that the value of
\mathrm{P}_{0}/\Phi_{0}
increases with the increase of axion charge, which is sensible since
\mathrm{P}
is related to charge fluctuations. Figure
7
shows the dependence on
\lvert c\rvert
. Since this parameter is proportional to the size of the wormhole, we can see that the eigenvalues are larger for macroscopic wormholes, while
\mathrm{P}_{0}/\Phi_{0}
has the same value for both microscopic and macroscopic wormholes.
